ParlaMint-DK_20160601090137	On behalf of the authors of the question, I should like to explain the question: at this time of the parliamentary year, it is a tradition for us to take a stand on the parliamentary year which has passed, and that is why we are asking tradition to ask the Prime Minister a question in which we are asking for an account of the domestic and foreign policy situation in which Denmark finds itself.
ParlaMint-DK_20160601090202	Thank you. Then there is an answer from the Prime Minister. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160601090236	Thank you so much. That is actually what I would like to begin by saying. Thanks to the Danish People's Party, thanks to the Social Democracy, the Radical Left, the Conservative People's Party, the Socialist People's Party, the Liberal Alliance, the Enhedslisten and the Alternative. With your help, we've been getting something out of hand since the election. Thank you. My ministers and I look forward to continuing cooperation for an even better Denmark. The government has come some way through broad cooperation • the broad cooperation, which is, of course, at the heart of our democracy. The opposition has also made proposals, some of them have found a majority, it is also democracy, but most importantly, the government has entered into agreements with all the parliamentary political parties, 38 in total, the first as early as the day after the formation of the government, and the latest yesterday; it is roughly a new agreement every 10 days. We expect 186 legislative proposals and resolutions to be adopted; this corresponds to close to a proposal a day during the parliamentary year. We have revived the tripartite agreements, which were otherwise declared dead and gone under the previous government. And I'm delighted about all this. I am particularly happy when I meet the commitment that keeps all of Denmark alive and going and brings us on to teachers, school leaders, fire-souls who help young people who got off on the wrong foot in adult life; relatives who do everything they can for their loved ones, and more; managers who hire refugees and contribute to better integration; jobs that open up to young people on the edge of society and give them a chance to gain control over their adult life. I have had visits by hundreds of dedicated Danes to the theme meeting and conference at Marienborg, and if any of you are listening here today, I would like to thank you for the inspiration. Next to you are the thousands all over the country who take responsibility every day and do something extra. Thanks for the effort. You make Denmark Denmark. The country we have today is not built by one party, one man or one woman. It was built by the Danes. Our country is not resting, not on easy solutions, but on responsible solutions. Therefore, Denmark is a very special country, a strong society of security, of prosperity, of harmony. We must take care of that in a world that changes, and we must improve that. Last summer, when the government stepped in, I put four points of reference for the government's work. Firstly, we need more private jobs. Secondly, we need a stronger core welfare. Thirdly, we need more people into the working community. Fourthly, we must pursue a consistent and realistic policy on foreigners. In all four areas, we are now less than a year away. More private jobs were, and are, my first point of reference, because private jobs are there, income comes from, that is what the welfare society is based on. Companies must have good conditions so that new jobs can be created in Denmark and not outside Denmark. We have relaxed NO x We reduce inheritance taxes. We have removed the advertising tax with which the previous government would burden companies. And together we have made tax cuts for 4 billion. kr. We are making life easier for businesses to stop burdens; targeted action against over-implementing EU rules; the aim is that new business legislation will only come into force twice a year. The regulation of our agriculture, an important part of the food cluster, had become too rigid. We have done this more sensibly so that the whole large food cluster will have better conditions so that they can create jobs, even where it is needed. The housing job scheme has been reinstated, so craftsmen have something to tear in, also out there, where there is a little longer between houses. We are liberalising the law of plans. I hope that later this week it will end happily with a broad agreement that gives more responsibility to those to whom the decisions relate and comes close. It will also help to provide activity throughout the country. We're doing more than that. We are in the process of the largest relocation of state jobs ever. 4,000 jobs are moving from the capital to the rest of the country. I was there myself when the Danish Trade Administration first opened in Ribe. Today, 4 months later, more than 650 jobs have been relocated and the vast majority follow this year and next year. We do this because we have to take responsibility for a Denmark that is linked. We have to believe that the whole country can take responsibility. That's why we're moving government jobs, and that's why we need more private jobs. That is also why I am rather sorry that a majority in the Folketing has decided to reduce the opportunities for companies to employ highly qualified foreigners. It's moving in the wrong direction. But that does not shake the fact that overall it is going the right way. More people have come to work. The new figures that came yesterday speak their very clear language. Since last summer, 38,000 more private jobs have been created. Without jobs no wealth, without prosperity no welfare. That's how simple the equation is. Therefore, my second point of reference was, and is, that we must have a stronger core welfare. I'm in favour of security for everyone, no matter what family you're born into. My own mother left school after 7th grade, I went after the university, and many in my own generation are examples of the same. You gotta be able to move. That's what I grew up in. This is what I want my children and grandchildren to grow up in. That is why I also attach great importance to the fact that there are good opportunities for choosing the right education. Education is the main road to moving socially • good education, right for the individual, useful for the community. We have presented an ambitious proposal for reform of the high schools. The aim is to strengthen the skills of students, because we are tightening the requirements and making upper secondary education better and more targeted. But high school is not the right way for all young people. We need more people to choose vocational training, preferably in the first attempt, so that they get the best possible starting point in adult life. I am a strong supporter of a secure welfare society. And what is more fundamental to security than being able to receive treatment when you are ill? It is my vision that citizens should have high quality health care, regardless of their postal code, regardless of their wallet. It was a vision that we started to realize back in 2001, and since then we have come quite far better cancer treatment, super hospitals, unique patients' rights but we haven't got far enough. We never really do that either, because we must always have the ambition to do the good even better. The government adds 2.4 billion. kr. extra for health this year • 2.4 billion. kr. That is exactly what we voted for, and that is 0.5 billion. kr. more than the previous government went to the polls. And here in the Folketing we have agreed on a number of principles for prioritising hospital medicine. Thank you for the broad support for that. We have adopted a new right of investigation, a right of inquiry, and this with a very broad majority behind it. Some of those who have always been opposed now suddenly voted in favour. Thank you. I think this is positive and I see it as an expression of a change of attitude. When 15 years ago I announced the idea that patients should have strong rights, that they should have the opportunity to choose the waiting time from whatever size of the purse, that one should be able to move from the queue, that one should also have an opportunity to choose private, if the queue is too long, yes, then it was an ideological battleground. It is so evident and fortunately not anymore. Thank you. Now we put the human being before the system, and the systems must be better at talking to each other. Too many older people and their relatives have tried to fall between two systems, and then you settle down. That is why we have set aside 1.2 billion. kr. to an action plan for elderly medical patients. We have implemented an emergency package aimed at the major problem that patients must lie on the corridors. We need the treatment to be connected across the hospital, the municipality and our own doctor for the benefit and pleasure of the patient. The next step will be to make Denmark a dementia-friendly country. Dementia is a disease that affects more and more people. It is a heavy disease to bear, and it is a very heavy disease to be related to. Imagine not being able to have a conversation with someone closest to you. Imagine that the man or woman you've been married to in a lifetime suddenly withers and becomes someone else. It causes heavy anxiety, it causes anxiety, it causes impotence. Three weeks ago I visited the apple garden community in Holte. Here I had a good talk with Aase and Mariann, who have both lost their men after illness with dementia. As Aase said, you get worn out. And when the nursing home is finally there and it is good that it can be the hardest moment anyway, because, as Mariann said, the worst is the day when you realize that he is not coming home again. The next of kin carry the heaviest burden. We owe them a strong hand so they don't fall under that burden. The government has set aside 470 million. kr. to a new national dementia action plan. We need to strengthen the core welfare. There must be money for that. It must be possible to afford this, even if the basic conditions of the public economy are clearly low. Despite this, the government, with the backing of the parties that have taken responsibility for the Finance Act, adds a total of 3.4 billion. kr. extra for health and the elderly this year. 2.4 billion kr. for health, 1 billion. kr. I would like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report. Despite a tight economy, the government has given the municipalities 1⁄2 billion. kr. more for service this year • 1⁄2 billion. kr. More. Under the former Social Democratic-led government, municipalities could spend 3 billion. kr. less so, even though it may not be the impression one gets from the debate. We need more people in the working community. That was and is my third pointer. Denmark is not economically linked when too few have to provide for many. Then it's not human. That is why we need to set up a society where it is better to work. The government has carried out an integration grant so that in future foreigners will receive what is equivalent to the young people's SU. We have reinstated a ceiling on cash benefits, with the aim of providing a substantial income increase for cash benefits recipients by taking a full-time job also to a wage at the low end. I think that is fair. And both are part of the first phase of the job reform that we chose to implement. The second phase is targeted tax relief on, in particular, small labour incomes. We will have to do something about this in the autumn so that work pays better. We must create a more efficient and flexible labour market. It is also an objective in the tripartite negotiations with employers and employees. When we called them and started them, I did not take it for granted that they would succeed, because four years ago they broke down, and that sort of wears off. Fortunately, however, the Danish model has proved to be hard-wearing and I welcome the fact that employees and employers have shown courage and socialism. We have now reached one agreement, and I expect more to come. The first agreement includes a new basic integration training, for the new ones among us, no matter how many people are expected to come, some people are not allowed to end up in public support. They're gonna make their own money, because if they don't, they're gonna have to live off ours. Therefore, it is part of the wider aim of the government to get fewer on transfers and more in work. In six months, the number of people on public support has fallen by about 10,000 people. It's going the right way, but we have to move on. We're not in the finish line. My fourth point was and is a consistent and realistic foreign policy. We need to control the influx of asylum seekers. The problem is complicated, the background is serious, the situation is unhappy. Last summer, Europe broke open. People fleeing from anything or fleeing to the dream of a better life flowed completely unhindered across the external borders, continued up through the countries, also to Denmark. We all remember the pictures of migrants and refugees who wandered on the highways from Rødby and up through Zealand. And from the outset it was clear that the solution has two dimensions: a European and a national one. Basically, it was, and indeed is, a common European problem that Europe had to solve together. However, to put it mildly, there were very different positions in the European Union, which is why the answers dragged out and dragged out, which is why, of course, we had to act ourselves. There was a need to tighten up after the previous government's 31 relaxations of immigration policy • 31 reliefs. I believe that the chairman of the Radical Left usually counts it as 45, but leave it at that. It was in all fairness 31 relaxations. We have introduced stricter requirements for family reunification for refugees with temporary protection, stricter rules for permanent residence, integration benefit, border control. The government is pursuing a consistent foreign policy. Last year, in Sweden, Norway, Finland and Germany, in all our neighbouring countries, the number of asylum seekers increased by 100 per cent. or more. In Denmark it increased by about 40%. That is too many, but much less than what we saw in our neighbouring countries. Imagine the situation where it wasn't tighter. The government's attitude has always been • also before we became government • that number matters. It's not something we invented for the occasion. We've been saying that for years. We must make a massive effort to control the external borders of the European Union effectively. Refugees are best helped in the neighbourhoods. We must pursue a realistic foreign policy. We cannot solve the world's refugee problems in Europe. We did not share that view with many people when Europe broke open. That was not something that all Member States wanted to hear. For a start, we spoke to the deaf when we talked about the fact that it is not a solution just to distribute the problem but that the problem needs to be solved. Now there has been greater responsiveness. The Balkan route is closed. The European Union has reached an agreement with Turkey. We can send back irregular migrants and asylum seekers. Cynical smugglers must give up making money from the fact that families and children are risking their lives in overcrowded and deceptive boats. In January up to 70,000 people came from Turkey to the Greek islands, in May about 1,300. We feel that in Denmark too. In January we received over 1,600 asylum seekers, in May about 350. It is far fewer than in the autumn, and it is the lowest number in a month since February 2012. That doesn't mean we can sit back. The power from Turkey can start again, and the traffickers can find new routes. However, this shows that when pressure is pressed, the European Union is able to act. This shows that Denmark is being listened to. This shows that strong European cooperation is in Denmark's interest. My position is clear: we must keep the number of asylum seekers to a reasonable level. And that means that we have to go down considerably further than both the 21,000 that came last year and the close to 15,000 that came 2 years ago. There we are now, but it must be supervised and the government is ready to act to ensure that it is not shaken. The world has moved closer with deeply complicated conflicts, with fragile states and terrorism. How do we best meet the new challenges and new opportunities? Last year the government asked Ambassador Peter Taksøe-Jensen to make some suggestions. Taksøe's report has already led to good discussions here in the Folketing, and I look forward to the continued discussions as we proceed with the individual recommendations. In Denmark we must take care of our safety and interest. We must look after our country; we must safeguard our values, at home and out in the world. We shouldn't open the door to anyone who doesn't want us to be okay. Therefore, we need lists of foreign hate preachers so that we can prevent them coming to Denmark. For those who are already here, we want to extend the criminalisation of statements that undermine Danish legislation and we refuse to be a laughing stock for our own money. That is why tax concessions and public information support are over for those who preach against our democracy. We reached a broad agreement on all this yesterday. Out in the world, we need to get rid of ISIL's reign of terror. This is central to achieving stability in Iraq, Syria and in the rest of the region, and it is also important for our own security in Europe. It's a long way to go. It won't be easy. But there are signs that the coalition is pushing ISIL into the defensive, and now we need to hold on to it. On the civilian side, Denmark is increasing its support for restricting foreign fighters to ISIL, countering propaganda, cutting funding and preventing the terrorist movement from gaining a foothold in liberated areas. On the military side, we engage with F-16 aircraft, special forces and with a transport contribution. And I'd like to sign for Parliament's broad support for it. It shows that we stand together when it comes to this. In the longer term, modern and functional fighter planes are necessary, both for our preparedness at home, but also for us to continue to participate in international missions. Therefore, the government has proposed that our almost 40-year-old F-16 aircraft, which is very soon pensionable, should be replaced by F-35 Joint Strike Fighter. I can understand those who take note of the fact that it costs a lot of money, because it does. It's a big investment. But it is an investment in Denmark, it is an investment in our security, it is an investment in that Denmark can always remain Denmark, and it is absolutely necessary for us to continue to carry out the same tasks as before. The material must be top, for it is top material, it is equal to man, and they are top. Denmark has some of the world's best soldiers. The Danish soldiers and our other missions are doing a unique job in Iraq, Afghanistan, Mali and Kosova. They deserve our deepest respect, and that is why I should like to express my heartfelt thanks to our sendees today. When the sentees return home, we need to help those who need help. It's all missing. We've gotten better at that. We started it back in 2009-2010 when we formulated the first veteran policy, but we have to keep looking at whether we can do better yet. Therefore, the government is completing a service check of the efforts of the veterans. Our senders have made greater sacrifices and risked more than anyone. That's why the Danish Parliament supports our soldiers. They deserve that. Thank you so much. Last week we had the annual debate about the Kingdom Community here in the Folketing. It was a good, lively and long debate that also showed that there is broad support among the parties for the community between our three countries. Greenland, the Faroe Islands and Denmark have together found responsible solutions to difficult problems. The Government supports Greenland's efforts to develop the raw materials sector and generate new revenues, and by the end of the week I expect us to adopt the bill that will allow Greenland to extract and export uranium. Together with the Faroe Islands and Greenland, we have completed the work of identifying border barriers in the Kingdom Community. The next step will be to remove the obstacles that restrict freedom of movement within the Kingdom community. Both the Faroe Islands and Greenland want to take over responsibility for new matters. It is in the Faroe Islands, for example. personal, family and inheritance rights. In Greenland it is the working environment. We respect that; it strengthens autonomy. The Kingdom community is something special. I believe that we need to do more to mark our community, to respect diversity, to show that we appreciate each other, and that is why, as everyone knows, the government has decided that the Greenlandic and Faroese flags should be flagged on the national days of the countries on 21 June and 29 July. I'm looking forward to it. I'm proud of the Kingdom. I am also proud of the part of the Kingdom community called Denmark. Denmark is a great country. I can't, no matter how much I let the imagination fly, imagine a better place to live my life. I myself have had greater and richer opportunities than my parents, who again had a better starting point than my grandparents. It creates a duty to deliver a Denmark to our children, who are freer, richer and fairer than what we took over ourselves, namely a country that is still one of the richest and safest in the world, where a Danish family can afford the same as a Swedish or German family, can take on a holiday, help the children a little while on the way when they leave home, change the small car in the garage or carport, when the old one is worn down • things that matter in everyday life, the close well-being of the families. It is a country where core welfare works and is up-to-date, with good education and safe care, world-class health, a strong common welfare. I think most of us want that, if not what I both believe and hope • all of us. We have a responsibility, all of us, all of us Danes, but especially those of us who sit in this Chamber today. Denmark is the country of opportunity. Every year I have the pleasure and the honour of giving a prize to a person who has managed to reach out for opportunities, lift himself up and take responsibility for his own life despite opposition. It happens under the auspices of the Løkkefond, a project for boys on the edge that I helped start a few years ago. In the spring, I handed the prize as the Knight of the Year to Jamil. Jamil's starting point from school was • and it's not an exaggeration • not flashy, let's just put it that way, but it was. Through hard work, persistence, growing faith in his own abilities and discipline, he passed the 9th grade with 7- and 10-s and is now in his dream education as a carpenter. I think it is a good thing that it brings enormous respect. Jamil and many with him are the face of us all pursuing our dreams, and that it is possible to grow when faced with challenges and expectations. We must pursue those dreams as people, and we must as a country. We must make a strong Denmark stronger. Right now unemployment is falling, employment is rising, the sun is shining, it is actually going very well, we are not in a deep crisis as we were a few years ago. Last time I was prime minister, Denmark had been hit by the biggest international economic crisis since the 1930s, it was not that easy to overlook, and out of it there was also a drive forward, a little faster in some than others, but it grew. We carried out an unemployment benefit reform and we changed the post-retirement salary. It was major reforms, major reforms that stood, even though the government changed, and that means that as a country we are stronger today. After all, those reforms were possible because there was a strong sense of crisis, which is why there was support for, or at least an acceptance for, something to be done. That is the way it is: recognition is the first step towards action, not with easy answers, because the easy answers are rare, or perhaps never, but with thoughtful and responsible solutions, and that is why we must recognise our situation today. We're not in crisis, but the world is changing. Some would say that it has always been, and that argument actually has a certain right. Already 2,500 years ago, the Greek philosopher Heraklit pointed out that the only constant change is, and today we can add that the changes are happening at a pace that we have never seen before, in a way that we have not known and to an extent that affects all parts of society, the way we live, the way we work and the way companies compete. Globalisation, new technology and digitalisation merge old industries and give birth to new ones, everything changes. It opens up new opportunities, and we must be ready to seize them. We must act and, as I see it, there are seven challenges for Denmark. Firstly, we are not good enough to create growth and jobs. We do not get enough out of our work, we need to get more done, and no, that does not mean that we need to stress more or that we need to run faster, but that means that we need to be completely advanced in our shoes and exploit robotics and digitalisation and train for business needs. For it is a fact that productivity in Denmark is growing more slowly than in all other countries in Europe, except Italy and Spain. It is a fact that too many investments land on the other side of the Øresund or south of the border instead of in Denmark. It is a fact that all Danish companies with over 1,000 employees are more than 20 years old. Secondly, the balance between those who are dependent and those who work is unbalanced. It is a challenge we have had for many years and it hangs, among other things. along with a high tax on work and rather generous transfer income. Thirdly, public expenditure is under pressure. The price of oil is low and we get less revenue from the North Sea, much less. Since we joined the government, the state's oil revenue has been adjusted by just under 9 billion. kr. in 2020, which means we have just under 9 billion. kr. less to do good with. It is almost as much as we spend on the police every year, and if you see it in a slightly longer perspective, the loss is even greater. Since 2013, the figure is double. Fourthly, the migration and refugee crisis. The cost of refugees must not run out of hand, because then we simply have less money for other things, in addition to the social, value and norm challenges. How much less money, indeed, depends on the number of people who come here, what conditions we offer them, and to a large extent on whether they can support themselves. These are all areas in which the government has taken action and where we will take action, for the sake of the economy, for the sake of coherence and for the sake of our values. Fifthly, we are also challenged on the common core welfare, and here is just one example: we have done much and improved cancer treatment, but a Dane with cancer is living less and less time than a Swede with cancer. We must improve core welfare at a time when public finances are tight. They are now, and they will be when the big vintages withdraw from the labour market, i.e. those who, like me, were born in the 1960s. In addition to these five challenges, we have two very acute problems which do not affect our overall economy in the same way, but which we still have to address, and that will then be the sixth challenge, namely that the current property assessments are not good enough. We're gonna need some more accurate assessments. An unresolved problem has been brought to us by the previous government. It must be solved so that the property owners can be safe with the treasure now and in the future. Seventhly, we must find another way of financing renewable energy. We're gonna make a green switch. Denmark is a green leader nation, that is what it is, and that is how it should be, but the way we have financed the green transition does not last. The PRS tax is contrary to EU rules. Moreover, it is also linked up so that it is very expensive for Danish companies and families, and that is why we have a clear and bound choice. We can open the PRS tax and start sending Danish taxes abroad. In my view, it will be Ebberød Bank or we can abolish the PRS tax and move the expense into the Finance Act and collect the same money. That is why the government has proposed a neutral change, just as we have said in the government base that we would do it, and our proposal makes no significant difference in terms of whether it is worthwhile to work or not in terms of equality and inequality; the bill is not getting any bigger; on the contrary, we want to reduce the bill. It is a challenge that must then become seven challenges, and it may seem heavy on the calendar this year's first summer day, which is why I would like to stress in conclusion that I do not want to dig into a black hole, on the contrary. My message today is that we have a strong starting point for making Denmark stronger if we act in time. We must take due care. If we don't do something, yes, then the future looks a little sad. If, on the other hand, we implement decisions and create new room for manoeuvre, then we will have brighter opportunities, and therefore the answer to the seven challenges I have listed here will be included in the government's plan for Denmark, the government's 2025 plan, which we are putting forward after the summer break. Denmark is a country with great opportunities if we look the problems in the eye and solve them together. We must take that responsibility upon ourselves, all of us here in the Folketing, and especially those who represent many voters. When you are a large party that wants to make a difference, you have to take co-responsibility, whether you are in government, support party or opposition. My own party has been involved in many agreements under the previous government: a tax reform, two major growth packages, education reforms and a financial agreement. We accepted the hand that was raised, and I would also like to raise my hand here today for cooperation in the autumn. We need broad agreements here at Christiansborg, that is my ambition, and I believe that. We must improve the opportunities of the Danes towards the next generation. We must make a strong Denmark stronger. The starting point is good, and it is far better than a year ago, thanks to resolutions in this Chamber. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160601094611	On Friday, March 25, Vivi Nielsen met at work. She met at the same place of work that she had had for more than 25 years, well liked by colleagues and the residents with whom she worked. The working day was the last hours of Vivis' life, because in a medicine room during the day Vivi Nielsen is stabbed from behind by a resident at Lindegården. This year we've lost Vivi. In 2014, two employees lost their lives on the Saxenhøj Bo offer. In 2013, an employee was killed at Ringbo in Bagsværd, and in 2012 it was a female social educator at Blåkærgård who did not come home from work. Five employees in the social psychiatry alone have paid the highest price. There is no reasonableness in this, and so we must not forget them. Because we have a responsibility here in the Folketing. There's let's find a solution before we go on summer vacation. We owe it to Vivi. We owe it to our colleagues, and we owe it to the survivors. Denmark is one of the world's strongest and most equal societies. We have a welfare society that works well in many places and guarantees people both fundamental freedom, security and development, but there are also problems: bureaucracy, lack of leadership, too few social progress, inequality in health and citizens who face a treatment that we simply cannot be aware of. The modern welfare society with free and equal access is for us Social Democrats at the heart of the development of future Denmark, but new country must be won. We need more people to break the negative social legacy. The level of education must be raised and there must be both money and time for care and care. In other words, we must have higher ambitions when it comes to each other. New social progress must be ensured. The parliamentary year is about to end. It's time to take stock. In the elections, a healthy economy was passed on and there is reason to welcome continued progress in employment, but unfortunately growth is too low. Investment is too low. We're starting to lack manpower in many places. The overall economy is under pressure. Of course, there are no simple answers, but for us there is something more crucial than anything else. First of all, it is about the justice that the Prime Minister did not find the basis for talking about. Secondly, we need prosperity. We must be able to create more private jobs. Thirdly, on accountability. We don't need more money than we do. Fourthly, to prioritize wisely and to recognise already now that, of course, the time is not for top tax incentives. Finally, to believe in the community that is in Denmark, and not least also in the public employees. Rich men have systematically moved their money away from Denmark to tax havens on the other side of the globe. Those who have saved their fortunes in Panama have not fulfilled the part of the contract that we each have to fulfil. That's why we have to respond in cash. We in the Social Democratic Party have presented an ambitious plan against tax evasion. Today's message from my side is, get a call for negotiations. We need to ensure both a more transparent and fair tax system, because how are we going to keep asking the Danes to pay their taxes when the government is at the same time passive to those who cheat the community? Yesterday we were reminded that growth is stabbed. It has to be said that the government's enthusiasm for zero growth has really come through. Denmark has a zero growth government. If we are to make new progress, we need strong companies. That is why it was a mistake when the government spent a great deal of money on the housing job scheme, lowering the registration fee and the inheritance tax. Because none of the three things really help the Danish companies. We already know that we are going to lack labour: skilled employees, not least employees with vocational training. The fact that today the old Lindøværft is back on track is due to our climate ambitions. The fact that the harbour up in Hirtshals is among the most modern on ferry transport is due to the green transition. If we want jobs throughout Denmark, also for our unskilled, then we must cultivate the Danish positions of strength, because it is when we combine our Danish values and our skill that we can win in the global competition that we cannot by turning down our ambitions. That is why, of course, it is also the wrong way to go when the government chooses to cancel the Togfonden DK and slow down the green transition. The government has tried to make Togfonden DK a huge hole in the treasury. We now know that's not true. That's 200 million. kr. on a yearly basis, in terms of permanent revenue. We have a desire to bind Denmark as a whole together, and we will therefore propose that we take 200 million by 2020. kr. per year from the pool to better and cheaper public transport. The funding for the Train Fund DK will then be incorporated into a future 2025 plan. In this way we can bind Denmark both responsibly and fairly together. It goes without saying that we should never spend more money than we have. Just as right it is to help people flee in today's greatest refugee crisis, it is equally true that we cannot accept more than we can integrate, both because the cultural challenges of gender inequality and parallel societies need to be addressed, but also because otherwise it would mean huge savings elsewhere. So we must maintain a strict immigration policy, but at the same time give priority to stronger international engagement in the neighbourhood. At home, too, we must give priority, but not with a re-priority contribution, because that word covers nothing but savings. It goes without saying that everywhere in our society we must become more skilled, including in our welfare societies. But we want to spend the money where the Danes meet the welfare, not cut it down. When you put all this together, the time is simply not for blue block tax cuts. We will be discussing our overall economic policy in the autumn. I wonder if blue blocks' creativity extends to making worn-out access to pre-retirement and folk pensions even more difficult, to give carrots at the top and whip at the bottom and otherwise to cut down on what we have built up? As always, we will be involved in making the important decisions, but it must work. After all, Denmark has not become a rich country on top tax incentives or on reducing the access of hfs to universities. Denmark has not become one of the world's most secure societies on the back of great social inequality, and Denmark has not become a country with great cohesion through conflicts and pointless block policy, whether it be blue or red. We have a government that pushes all the important decisions ahead of it and at the same time vacuums all the corners of our society to scrape together for tax cuts. It is short-sighted, it is wrong, and excuse me, the government is too foot-to-mouth: no growth initiatives, no action against tax fraud, no solution for the public employees who are at risk of putting their lives at risk, no infrastructure for the whole country. In short, Denmark needs to get the boilers back on track not blindly by pumping tax relief into the economy here and now, not by blindly cutting back on those who already have the least. We need to look further, ensure that more people are able to become more skilled, because it is, at the end of the day, ourselves, our hands, our heads and our community that will ensure our common future. Then I would like to read out a proposal for adoption. Proposal for adoption The Danish Parliament notes that after a year of power the government has not delivered on its own four success criteria or delivered the long-term solutions that Denmark needs. The Danish Parliament invites the government to set a new course of cooperation broadly within the Danish Parliament. Parliament notes that the development of the welfare society requires that the re-prioritisation contribution be abolished in order to ensure core welfare throughout the country. The Danish Parliament also notes that there is a need to pursue a policy that ensures equal opportunities, combats poverty in Denmark and inequalities in education and health, and sets high ambitions for the environment and climate. • V 80).

ParlaMint-DK_20160601133310	Thank you, President. I would actually like to use this opportunity to tell a story about Susanne, a woman with whom I have had contact in recent years. When Susanne was a child, she fell 7 m from a tree and broke most bones in her body. Fortunately, she was put back together, but has since been persecuted by the consequences. She's gonna have to be run over, and she's in chronic pain. Yet Susanne struggled for an education and actually had good jobs in different companies for a while. She had children, lived in a house north of Copenhagen, but unfortunately the pain got worse and worse, and the operations also increased, and eventually she could no longer do a job. After a period of sickness benefit, she entered the cash benefits system and, as you know, other rules apply. To receive cash benefits, she had to sell her house, and she and the children had to move to a social housing estate outside Copenhagen. Her doctor and all other experts do not believe that she will ever regain her ability to work, and they think she is entitled to an early retirement pension. But the municipality is of a different opinion, so Susanne, like thousands of other Danes, has ended up in a limbo in the cash assistance system. Too sick to work, but according to the municipality's opinion she is too healthy to receive early retirement. Life on cash benefits, unlike what you sometimes hear in here, is tough. There is never money at the end of the month, there is no money to go to the movies with the kids, and the debt to family and network just grows. Susanne's first great grief when she lost her job was the loss of working capacity and identity and colleagues, but afterwards came the shock of meeting a system that was actually thought to help you, but which instead met her with suspicion and bureaucracy and sluggishness. On top of that, the shame came from the prejudiced debate on the unemployed, to which many of my colleagues in this House also contribute, and from the fear of the next newspaper cover or the next documentary programme about the lazy unemployed. Susanne's story is just one of many we hear them sadly over and over again about how people die before their retirement cases get treated, about sick people sent in so-called bed practice. Many of these people, like Susanne, have been working and contributing to society for years, and they are deeply shocked by the conditions they face when they have been innocuously affected by unemployment or disease. We discover it only when it hits ourselves, i.e. how short the distance has been from a normal life to an economic deroute; a note of fire, an accident can change the whole life in a short time, because security is no longer there. These conditions have not come about on their own; it is the consequence of two decades of social policy that has had the clear objective of making life as unemployed as hard and as unpleasant as possible. That is the consequence of the so-called reforms that a majority of the old parties in here have adopted across the blocs. The story of Susanne is a story about Denmark, as Denmark has become, but why? The reforms are necessary, we were told, because we need more growth. But growth is lower today than before the majority in this House went through reform, and yet they are now calling for new reforms, that is, more insecurity, more insecurity for ordinary people, more of a medicine that has obviously not worked. However, even if the reforms actually created growth, we may have to ask ourselves what price we are actually prepared to pay for marginally higher growth. Are we prepared to accept a country with significantly higher inequality? Are we prepared to spend less time with our families? Are we prepared to expose our unemployed colleagues to even more humiliation? Are we prepared to sacrifice our climate and our environment? We have never had that discussion, and we should take that discussion. The second argument put forward by the Reformers is that the reforms were necessary if we were to be able to afford welfare in order to create room for manoeuvre, as is so well known in the lingo of the old power parties? But how is this connected, because Denmark is one of the world's most prosperous societies, and we have become increasingly prosperous over the last few decades? It is true that the wealth we have has been distributed more and more unequally, because more and more wealth is concentrated in a few. For there is also another Denmark far from Susanne, far from all the other Danes who are affected by unemployment and illness, far from all ordinary Danes. This is the Denmark that the Prime Minister had invited the other day to a meeting about at Marienborg, i.e. about Denmark's future • Director Denmark, Millionærdanmark. And there is certainly nothing missing here, because while ordinary people's wages have stood still since the financial crisis, pensioners and unemployed people have seen their incomes fall, but Director Danmark has seen significant income increases. There is no money for welfare, they say. Okay, but there was money to give Goldman Sachs 10 billion Danish kroner for 18 months' service. There is no advice, they say, who are not able to afford decent conditions for vulnerable Danes, but there is money to spend 50 billion. kr. on new fighter planes. There is no advice, they say, who are no longer able to afford a community of solidarity, but who can afford to reduce corporate tax by 4 billion. kr., to lower the top tax by 3 billion. kr. And to put the treasure down on luxury sports cars. The reality is that there are the so-called reforms were not necessary. They were political choices and they were wrong political choices. Some new political choices are needed in Denmark, there is a need for courage, there is a need for innovation, and change is needed. We do not need any more such reforms, which are just another word to make life safer for ordinary people. We don't need any more leg buckles for Susanne and the other exposed Danes. We don't need more gifts for those who already have more than they can use. No, we need a determined effort to combat inequality, and we could appropriately start by ensuring that the banks that have made billions of dollars to be saved by the state pay back to the community crown for crown. But innovation is not just about the economy; it is also about trust, because it is not only Susanne and the other unemployed Danes who are faced with mistrust, it is also the people who take care of our welfare societies. Instead of trust and faith in their professionalism, our public employees are being drained with documentation requirements and schedules, for imagine if the SOSU helper took a cup of coffee too much with Mrs Sørensen, or think if the social worker called the unemployed person for a check-up too little. Let us now give up the crazy idea that the math boys inside the Treasury and all their Excel sheets understand the world better than the people who stand with their hands in the fuck. Our skilled nurses, educators, teachers, social workers must meet with confidence and recognition, not with control and detailed management. Let us then also show confidence in our local democracy, in our municipalities, and let them manage their own economy instead, as the government wants, come and take the money out of the municipal coffers with the so-called down-priority contribution. But our role in the world also calls for change. In the last year, the perception of Denmark in the world has, frankly, not improved. Newspaper ads to keep refugees away, a jewelry law that shocked the whole world. There was a time when Denmark was known to be a pioneer country, which aroused awareness of the good, for its progressive attitudes. Imagine if Denmark again became known as a progressive and peaceful nation, as world champion of development aid, as a conflict-solver, as a bridge-builder. That's our vision. But perhaps the most important change is about our future on the planet. It has been truly sad to see a government struggling to undermine the green transition. One day they pass a manure law, which will very likely lead to oxygen and dead fish in our fjords and streams, the next day they will stop the construction of wind turbines. We're going the opposite way. We're not going down. We're going up. We need to adapt to a future where what grows and grows is our leisure time and our quality of life instead of our material consumption. It's an opportunity, not a problem. More time for family and shorter working hours is something that many of us dream of. It is not natural law that Susanne should live under the conditions she does, or that wealth should clump together with a few at the top. It is political decisions they can change, Denmark can become a good country, where everyone is with us and where we look out for each other. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20160601142915	Denmark is a lovely country for which we live and breathe. It should be good to live here, it should be good to grow up here, here it should be good to do business. That is the way it should be in the future. However, Denmark is facing two major crises: the growth crisis and the migration crisis. If we want, we can get Denmark out of both crises, but it takes more than fine words. It requires action, it requires will, and it requires the courage to make decisions too. The Liberal Alliance supports the current government, but let it be said immediately: the government's finest achievement so far is enough that it has hutted through the Folketing for a whole year. Well, we are happy with some of the results that have been achieved, such as: lower registration fee, lower NOx tax, lower inheritance tax, ceiling on housing taxes, better opportunity for remuneration with employee shares, increased support for free and private schools, securing fixerums for drug addicts and many other good things. But if we look at what is really batting, namely to restart the Danish economy and ensure that we manage the migration flow, then expectations have been greater than the results. It is well said that the joy of expectation is the greatest of all, and my goodness, we rejoice in the Liberal Alliance. For what an exciting autumn we face. Tax and reform must be negotiated that can kick off growth, and the ambition of the Liberal Alliance is to be like the prince of the white reformer who comes and saves the welfare princess of her thorny sleep. A vanet bear has grown high, but we are not going to wait for 100 years. The Liberty Sword is to free the princess and she and all the queen kingdom must experience growth, prosperity and welfare to the joy of women, men and children. The Danes are a friendly people. We should be happy about that. But sometimes we get so friendly that we just smile and pretend. We imagine and pull our mouths up and think ours. One cannot help being happy when one is told that we belong to the happiest people in the world. Even though the individual may be upset, exhausted, stressed, depressed, or quite unhappy. We are also delighted when we are told how fantastic a country Denmark is to operate in the OECD even though there are more countries in the OECD than I have fingers and toes on my hands and feet that have higher economic growth than Denmark. Because we're not alone friendly. We are also wary of conflict, and who will destroy the good atmosphere? I have to apologize in advance, but I will. Because never has any country been so high on so many lists and got so little out of it. Yes, we celebrate one top ranking after another on all the charts of the world. But for some reason it never really translates into growth, prosperity, and welfare. Denmark has had the fifth lowest growth in the OECD since 1995, and it doesn't look much better when we look out on the horizon. OECD predicts that Denmark will have the sixth lowest growth by 2030. So it is essential that we are in a crisis of growth. That is why we need to get the reforms under way. The tax on work must be reduced and we must have met the Government's ambition to lower the top tax. Furthermore, the Liberal Alliance has an ambition that no one who goes to work should pay tax on the first DKK 7,000 he or she earns per month. No one can be expected to contribute to supporting others until one can support oneself. At the same time, we want to ensure that working pays better. The difference between a low-wage job and passive support should be greater, and finally there is an easy fruit to pick: Danes have to retire a little later than they do today. We want to remove bureaucracy and rules so that it becomes easier for the Danes to live the life they want and create growth to the delight of themselves and the common. From the end of the 1800s until the crisis, Denmark had an economic growth of an average of 2%. a year. From 2008 to 2015, the Danish gross domestic product decreased by 0.4 per cent. Over the same period, Sweden's GDP increased by 11%. We need to get Denmark into a higher growth gear. We can if we want. It requires only 90 mandates in this high thing, and the Liberal Alliance is happy to deliver the 13 first and look forward to negotiating with our partners in the autumn so that we can deliver solutions for the benefit of the Danes. The Danes are not alone facing a growth crisis. We are also facing a migration crisis. Of course, we are not alone in this. There is a terrible civil war going on in Syria that has sent millions of people to flee. There are other millions from Africa and Asia who want a better life and have set themselves in motion against Europe. But the whole world cannot live in Europe and half the world cannot live in Denmark. In 2014, 14,792 asylum seekers came to Denmark. In 2015, 21,225 asylum seekers came to Denmark. This year the government is budgeting 25,000 asylum seekers. Of course we must help, but more people are coming and applying for asylum in these years than Denmark can handle. That is why the Liberal Alliance has proposed a humane moratorium on asylum. It means a stop in order to be able to seek asylum spontaneously in Denmark itself for 2 years. During the same period, 4 000 UN quota refugees are to be taken a year, and the many saved expenses in the area of asylum will be used to help in Syria and in the vicinity of Syria, people who need protection, people who are starving, children who need schooling. In this way, we can help far more people. It is the most humane thing, and we can ensure that Denmark does not have to integrate more than we can overcome. The ideal solution is, of course, still European. We want to strengthen the EU's external borders significantly, but until sustainable and lasting European solutions are created, national solutions are needed. If we hesitate, there will be a great deal to pay; specifically for the municipalities, specifically for more people outside the labour market, and specifically for more integration problems. The good news is that, as with the growth crisis, we can also resolve the migration crisis in Denmark, if we want to. This requires new thinking, and it requires the courage to take unconventional decisions: tax relief on top and bottom, a humane asylum freeze. These are the most important issues facing Denmark. These are the most important cases in the autumn. The Prime Minister has said that it is not possible to solve all Denmark's problems by lowering the top tax or by reducing the number of asylum seekers. That is absolutely correct. But it helps. Oh, my God! But I agree with the Prime Minister that we should not settle. More reforms are needed, so Denmark becomes a country in growth and harmony, so Denmark becomes a country that young people from all over the world want to try to live in, because this is so great. We can do that if we want to. The Liberal Alliance will. We are ready to negotiate with good ideas and solutions. We are ready to take responsibility. We are playing with open cards, and now we are waiting for the government to play out and show that together we can deliver solutions to the problems of the growth crisis and migration crisis for the benefit of the Danes.

ParlaMint-DK_20160601151718	Today's debate marks not only the end of the parliamentary year, but also the end of the first year of the Alternative as a political party here at Christiansborg, and this naturally gives rise to a lot of reflection, both about cooperation, about working form, but also about the past and about the future. I think I speak for both myself and the rest of my party colleagues when I say that it has been as exciting and challenging a year as we could have imagined in advance. Most of us came directly from the street and into the Folketing without the shadow of political experience, which is why we have had a very steep learning curve. In this respect, I would like to thank all those who have helped with everything, from finding their way around the Castle to the work of the committees and here in the Chamber. It seems almost forever since now, but the truth is that a great deal has happened with the alternative, yes, with Denmark, since we launched the parliamentary season in October. If I am going to try to sum up how the Alternative has experienced the year, then I would say that at one and the same time it has been a year that has both filled us with great, great concern, but also with hope for the future. Our concern is that the direction the government, together with its support parties, has set for Denmark, is heading us in a completely different direction from that which we want in the alternative. We want to steer Denmark in a direction of serious, sustainable conversion, so that Denmark will not only be the best country in the world, but the best country for the world. It may just sound like big words, but we have never concealed the fact that we may be a small party, but we have great, great ambitions. We have ambitions to create a greener, more entrepreneurial and more equal society, a society where we move away from the narrow box thinking of the competitive state to human and environmental thinking and where we work seriously both with an economic, a social and an environmental bottom line, but where we are aware that the environmental and social bottom line is the goal, while the economic will always be only the means to reach the others. That is the society we dream of, and that is what we work for every day within and outside Christiansborg's walls. Unfortunately, the last year suggests that the government has chosen a completely different direction for Denmark. This is a direction in which re-priority contributions are introduced which downgrades the close well-being of implementing a ceiling on cash aid, which increases inequality and makes vulnerable children and families poorer, but it is also a direction in which we do not take enough responsibility for the two major challenges: the climate crisis and the global refugee challenge. If we start with the climate crisis, science is ready: temperature increases must be so if the climate crisis is not to become a permanent climate disaster. That is why the Paris Agreement marked for us a turning point which says that the fossil industry is on the wrong side of history, but the agreement is also a gun to the temple because it quite clearly says that it is now that we must act if we are to save the climate. Regardless of how you turn it around, as a society, we need to recognise that growth has its limitations, because we cannot continue to use resources without limits when we live on a planet with limited resources. We have to acknowledge this, including in the policy pursued. We already see and feel the consequences of letting go. January was the warmest month ever measured. February was the warmest month ever measured. So was March, and so was April. And yet it does not seem that the scale of the crisis has come to the fore. For example: The keys to the Prime Minister's Office were barely in the hands of the Prime Minister before the government defied a majority in the Folketing and raised doubts about Denmark's reduction targets. Then a regular bomb was dropped into the electric car market when the tax exemption was abolished, just as the farmers were suddenly allowed to spray and fertilize more. The last thing we have heard is that the black record list now needs to be extended again, because the government is proposing to drop the PRS tax. We do not think that makes sense in any way when we know that these initiatives are working against the green transition at a time when there has never been a greater need for us to speed up that transformation. Denmark's handling of the global refugee challenge is another example that worries the alternative. The challenge of refugees is too great for any country to take up the task alone. I'm pretty sure we all agree on that in here. That is why we are surprised that Denmark was one of the first countries to reject the Commission's proposal for a pan-European distribution key, because if we all agree that common solutions are needed, why on earth do we reject it when it lies ahead of us? After all, we do not solve the refugee challenge by tightening legislation at home. It merely passes the problem on to our neighbouring countries, and we do not think that is a solidarity or a sustainable solution. Speaking of refugees, I would like to make a brief comment on the work process we had in the Folketing when we had to deal urgently with the government's asylum package. It may be because we are brand new in the game in here, but we just don't think it's okay that we Members get a wretched day to understand a law of more than 100 pages. That is why we would like to call on the government to ensure a better process when we make such a great piece of legislation in the future. As I started by saying, the year that has passed not only fills the alternative with concern, but also with hope. This is due not least to the fact that some small progressive enclaves have begun to emerge around Denmark, experimenting with new ideas, new communities and new solutions to the challenges and crises we face now and in the long term. One of the very best examples is on Samsø, where the island's inhabitants have joined forces to create a sustainable local environment, so that today you are both self-sufficient in renewable energy and move towards future-proof agriculture based on ecology. In Lysbro there is a local savings bank offering green loans, eco-loans and humanitarian training loans. Such communities would like to have many more of them in the future. It gives a hope of a sustainable society that local responsibility for the green transition begins when the political will can be difficult to see. Hopefully, we will also be in the alternative when we see how civil society moves in the area of refugees and integration. Organisations and individual volunteers have played and continue to play an invaluable role in integration. We see how companies, welcome families and friendly people take a huge responsibility for local integration, and we see how new efforts and collaborations arise between municipalities and private and local associations and organisations. These bets are worth gold, both literally and figuratively. The faster refugees become part of society, the better. And we all know that it is first and foremost in the meeting between people that the best integration takes place. That is why the alternative would like to praise the Prime Minister for inviting civil society to a talk about how we integrate, but at the same time we would like to appeal for civil society to have an even more prominent role when we try to achieve the world's best integration in the future. Now it was really my plan to stop, but before I finish altogether, I would like to say a little about how we have tried internally in the Alternative to create a new and more inclusive political culture. We have, for example. Trying to create new spaces for policy development and dialogue. Many have probably heard about our political laboratory, but we have also tried what we call the alternative parliament, where we ask citizens, professionals and politicians to develop new answers to current challenges, in sync with the fact that this is happening in this Chamber. We have also explored the potential of combining art, politics and activism, partly by having a house artist living there, partly by exhibiting politically motivated art like what you see down at the Palace Square. At the same time, we are constantly trying to be inspired by the outside world and our colleagues, all of you in here. In fact, we have become so many times. Most recently, this has been done by a proposal from the Liberal Alliance and Radicals to introduce a social card for people on transfer income. In fact, we have no doubt that there is plenty of innovation and inventiveness in here if we are willing to lower our guard and allow each other to be inspired by those who are sitting opposite us. In any case, we are ready to be inspired by you both by you in this Chamber and, of course, by you who are behind the screen. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20160601190018	Thank you. After all, it is a challenge to be the first speaker right after the food, where it is probably heavy and people may be tempted to sit around a bit. I'll try to keep the hall awake. We look back on an eventful parliamentary year, a year of many negotiations, a year of many agreements. I myself measure our progress on whether we have come closer to a society of security and opportunity. And I think we are in a number of areas. Before the election, Danish families experienced that living in their own homes became more expensive and expensive. The debt increased, SKAT's property assessments were skewed and too high, and neighbors in one's houses got different bills. It goes without saying that it was too bad. Citizens in Denmark should not be reduced to spectators in their own lives, where the state creates unpredictable and rising bills. The landowners should not be the prey of the state when tax father plays Nak & Eat. The reality was that at 75 years of age Kirsten, who had answered each his, was hit by a huge increase. She paid 38,000 kroner each year. I'm sorry, but the bill was over years to grow to 108,000 kroner. The property value tax comes on top of that. 108,000 kr. At the annual expense of a single pensioner, it is not something that provides security for one's home. As the retired Kjeld from Brønshøj said: I only have my pension, and we cannot stay in the house if the skies continue. But in general the political Denmark failed. One could not undo Kjeld to live at 97 m2 with his wife. You'd think there was an agreement to stop rising taxes. Yes, of course, many would say, but unfortunately no. It is very significant how we treat homeowners in this country. We look at it differently. We did the same at the time, and that is why we said that the increase in the root cause must be stopped. In the election campaign, we said the same thing, and after the election, we said the same thing. When we opened the parliamentary year we are about to end, I said here from this platform: "We have reached the point where an ordinary family in Rødovre pays 26,000 kroner a year for their parcel house. And there was the prospect that that bill would rise. Fortunately, it has succeeded in stopping the rising ground. With the 2016 Finance Act, we managed to stop at 750 million. kr. in tax increases to houseowners • barely 21⁄2 billion. kr. over the next four years. And it was even a nominal freeze. Thus, the root cause did not increase in line with price developments or anything else. Families got total security about the guilt. The bill doesn't increase with as much as a penny. So here I really must praise the government and the other parties that supported the agreement. We are very pleased about this, and I think it is a pity that the debate on this always ends in the debate, especially when you talk to the left, that it is the richest one has secured in one way or another. No, we've just ensured that ordinary people can be allowed to live in the house they've bought and paid for. But there is no need to rejoice yet, and there is no need to let the champagne corks jump. We are not in the end, because the matter is not the end of freezing the ground. That is why we have written a final proposal that removes the land and property tax altogether, because you should not pay tax from living in your own home. It's a place of comfort that you can understand. Just as there is a need for security about the dwelling, so there is a need for security on the street. That is why we are very pleased that over 1,100 new students will be admitted to the police school for the next two years, compared with 900 in the previous four years. This is a result which we are very pleased to see negotiated with the government and the other parties. There must be law and order, and the police must have the necessary resources. We're not there yet, but we're on the right track. I see the police as a key player when we have to shed light on Shadow Denmark, where police and fire trucks are overwhelmed with stones and small traders being blackmailed for money. We should set up mobile local police stations in the heavily burdened residential areas. Denmark must be one Denmark, and law and order must apply to the farthest corner of the smallest ghetto. We know that some young people are looking for communities in the wrong places. It can be in extreme, religious environments, but it can also be in a gang. But there are plenty of opportunities for healthy communities in Denmark, and there are plenty of educational opportunities. Therefore, we can say to Shadow Denmark's hard-boiled youth: we hope you do not just think of something sensible, we demand it, and we destroy the criminal life you thought you were going to have. At the same time, we have agreed with the government to take up a fight with hate preachers. They are cowards who dare not travel in holy war and therefore plague the good hearts of young people so that they can be used as puppets. I don't see why we should let such destructive forces into our country. I think we are heading in the right direction, even when it comes to growth and opportunities. The prerequisite for us to talk about a good welfare society is that we have a good and competitive business. That should be number one. 1 in the textbook. That is not always the feeling you get when you listen to the debate in this Chamber. Therefore, we have helped to abolish more taxes and focus on business conditions and to ensure better conditions for generational change in companies, because it is not reasonable for a daughter to be hit by a special tax and perhaps fire employees because she chooses to run her parents' business further. ParlaMint-DK_20160601195042	Thank you for the floor. Soon, a year has passed since the parliamentary elections. The first parliamentary year of this period runs out, and this gives us cause for reflection on the year that has passed. From a Greenlandic perspective, there is much that we can look back on, which has been positive. At the time of the formation of the government, the Prime Minister invited the North Atlantic mandates to discuss the part of the government which concerns the Kingdom. Many of Inuit Ataqatigiit's highlights are included and provide a very good basis for the work for Greenland and Greenlanders. It is very positive that the North Atlantic members are consulted and that the inputs we provide are listened to, because we know Greenland best after all. We also know, however, that there are a great many fine words in politics, but that it may be difficult to do so. Together we have a task between Greenland and Denmark in terms of strengthening and developing the Kingdom Community. We have a shared responsibility and a common interest in investing in the development of Greenlandic society, not least in the 32 areas that Denmark continues to deal with on behalf of Greenland. Whether you are talking about strengthening the judiciary, the long-awaited National Defence Office, better conditions for the police, for the municipal officers or for a strengthened defence, it is also about human resources. It is about the education and competence building of the people who are to help make our society safe. How can we ensure that Greenland, on the basis of the principle of subsidiarity, is dealt with in the district court in its vicinity, while at the same time increasing the professional knowledge of the district judges that is so badly needed? We know that it is difficult in practice, and that is why we must also dare to talk about new solutions, so that both subsidiarity and professionalisation go hand in hand in a good way that we can live with in Greenland. Children and young people must grow up in a secure environment and know that we have a secure and competent judicial society. After lengthy work from Inuit Ataqatigiit to highlight the need for a personal data law in Greenland, the law finally falls into place. The lack of data monitoring in Greenland's society is finally ending by the fact that the Data Protection Agency is to carry out annual supervision and publish a report in the future. The entry into force of the Personal Data Act in Greenland takes place 15 years after Denmark, and this is unfortunately not an isolated example of the fact that legislation for Greenland enters into force decades after Denmark has noticed well in areas which Denmark is responsible for. We also saw the same with the Children's Act, which dates back to 1962, which, following repeated calls from Inuit Ataqatigiit, is finally being brought up to date. We need to put an end to the discrimination for the future to ensure that the Danish Government as well as the updating of devices in Greenland when Danish laws are updated or enter into force. Greenlandic society is still developing, but the need for economic growth is still pressing. It is about creating growth for welfare, so that future generations continue to have the right to education and the opportunity for a good childhood and a healthy life. Growth to welfare is also about strengthening and improving the welfare society that we have and ensuring a sustainable future of the welfare society. Growth to welfare is about creating an economy in balance, also towards 2040, so that we ensure that the economy is on a stable basis. Creating a balanced economy requires necessary solutions and reforms, but at least as much new growth. Inuit Ataqatigiit has previously pointed to the need for reforms in the area of housing, taxation and welfare, increased employment promotion, among other things. For young people with high unemployment, as well as increased investment and efforts to raise the level of education in Greenland, all areas that continue to require necessary reforms to this day, which we have not yet seen. From the Inuit Ataqatigiits side, we believe that Greenland should look openly at a large number of the opportunities to create growth instead of staring blindly at uranium extraction as the only solution. IA would like to see new raw material projects developed, but we say no to uranium extraction and exports. Growth in Greenlandic society must be achieved through a growth-enhancing enterprise policy that contributes to the creation of new jobs. Foreign investors must be attracted by the establishment of a common Arctic investment fund, which Inuit Ataqatigiit has advocated. Naalakkersuisut will make proposals for setting up an investment fund this autumn, which Inuit Ataqatigiit looks forward to. The international awareness of the Arctic must help to strengthen and develop the focus on the potential of Greenland. An increased focus on Arctic research is important, which can be disseminated and used for the benefit of the Arctic population. We must adapt to the new reality that is emerging, with massive and visible climate change in the Arctic. Greenland must participate actively in research and in science. An idea is the establishment of an Arctic innovation hub in Greenland, where ideas and research can be developed, a centre that can be a breeding ground for new business initiatives. The case of the service contract at Pituffik, Thule Airbase, is nearing completion. In Inuit Ataqatigiit, we are particularly concerned that a new tender opens up the possibility that it will be an American company that will receive the contract and that its employees will primarily become American citizens, who, according to the defence agreement, will not have to pay income tax in Greenland. This will have massive economic consequences for Greenlandic society, where the total revenue from the base today amounts to 10%. of the tax revenues in Greenland. Inuit Ataqatigiit therefore expects that the tender documentation will at least ensure that employees in Pituffik pay taxes in Greenland and that apprenticeship and student places on the base are maintained. Often when we talk about the Commonwealth, there is talk of cooperation between the Danish government and the Naalaksuisut, but the focus should be on how we can ensure close coordination and involvement of Inatsisartut in the matters relating to Greenland. Unfortunately, during the parliamentary year here we have seen repeated examples that it was originally the intention of the government that the Folketing should deal with matters relating to Greenland before Inatsisartut had had the opportunity to make a statement. It is an injustice which is at odds with the law of self-government, an injustice which cannot be repeated in the coming parliamentary years. I am very pleased that there has been broad support in the Greenland Committee for the criticism that has been made by the Greenland Members of Parliament. All members of the Folketing are members on an equal footing according to the Constitution, and since Denmark still holds 32 key areas on behalf of Greenland, it is obvious that Greenland's members of parliament are invited to the relevant conciliation negotiations, for example. the defence settlement or the criminal justice settlement, which is of great relevance to Greenland. It strengthens both the political process and it makes sense that we, who have the most knowledge of the Arctic and Greenland, also participate and sit at the negotiating table in the issues relating to the Arctic and Greenland. That is why I am also pleased that Greenland's members of parliament are now invited to the budget negotiations. I think it is important to remember that this is an historic moment and it is a positive step in the right direction. Inuit Ataqatigiit is ready to work in a spirit of solidarity, constructive and solution-oriented way to develop Greenland in close cooperation with Denmark. We have clear common goals for our countries and for the community. We look forward to continued good cooperation in the coming parliamentary years. Qujanaq. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-DK_20160601202235	Thank you.
