Hopefully, we will soon see improvements. The kingdom community is very much in focus here in Denmark. This is pleasing, and we are very pleased because this focus has been lacking for a little too many years. The Taksøe report has really helped to identify some of the areas where Denmark has a potential. All the countries of the world as well as the European Union now have an Arctic policy in which they all point to the Arctic as an important focus area, which you want to be a part of and which you would like to see as a partner in the future. The Self-Government Act was passed 7 years ago. With it Greenland was given the right to declare itself self-employed when we want to. The fact that Greenland wants to be an independent nation is seen by the overwhelming support for the parties working for an independent Greenland. If Denmark is to be an Arctic great power in the long term, it is therefore necessary that an independent Greenland still wants to be part of the Kingdom Community. We are aware that an independent Greenland will need allies. That is how it is for all small countries, including Denmark. The Danish economy is an open economy, where you are largely dependent on international trade. The opening up to the world is also taking place in Greenland, and with this opening comes as a natural focus that we must take care of our particular language and culture. With reference to the fact that many research books and research articles are in English, the Radicals suggested that the teaching should also take place in English so that students could become even more internationally oriented than they are today. The Danish People's Party shot down De Radicale's proposal and proposed instead of a tax on English-language advertisements to protect the Danish language. Although it can be seen as counterpoles, the points of both parties are important for a small nation like Denmark. On the one hand you are totally dependent on having to work internationally, and on the other hand the Danish culture and language are worth preserving. We are in the same process in Greenland. Naalakkersuisut has just proposed that the Greenlandic primary school should make English the first foreign language. Such a transition is a longer process, but a necessary process, because we need to open up to the world. This is necessary for our cooperation with mining companies from a number of countries around the world, and it is necessary to develop our tourism. The population is getting ready for a world and collaborators also outside Denmark. That is why this change in priorities. Through a broad settlement in Inatsisartut, we have adopted an ambitious development of our airport structure. In order to develop Greenlandic tourism, it is necessary to make the infrastructure more flexible. If tourists cannot get to the different parts of Greenland, no one will invest in hotels and experience industries in the areas. A large number of foreign investment banks and investment companies want to get involved in the Arctic. That's good news. This is among other things. The Nordic Investment Bank, which has allocated 500 million since New Year's time. Euros, just under 3.8 billion. DKK, for long-term investments in the Arctic regions. This requires, of course, close cooperation between Inatsisartut and Naalaksuisut. It also requires a change of mentality or a change of behaviour to rely more on foreign investors, such as funds, capital funds and pension funds. It is good to find new forms of cooperation and new areas where we can strengthen each other. Recently, during the debate on the Community report, I highlighted, among other things: closer university cooperation on several courses of study, increased cooperation in the business sector on knowledge sharing and investment, as well as the solution of the defence's future tasks in Greenland. However, it is at least as important to ensure that cooperation in the Kingdom community works today and, unfortunately, there are a number of areas that do not work as they should. If Denmark believes that the kingdom community should also in the long term together be an Arctic great power, it must understand the importance of fulfilling its responsibilities also in Greenland. The emergency aid in Greenland is not too good. We are still waiting for the defence report to show how to ensure the safety of Greenlandic citizens, the marine environment, tourists, employees and air passengers who may need it. We've been waiting a long time for that report. By September it will have been 3 years since the Reichsaudit made the spectacular criticism of the preparedness in Greenland, and Greenland is still waiting for action. It is particularly striking that the defence has said that there is no means of dealing with the preparedness for a proposed mining project and that therefore the mine itself must finance the preparedness. That is far from acceptable. It also seems that we in Greenland will be waiting a long time for precise charts. As a number of key employees have not wanted to move with the Spatial Data Board to Aalborg, the government will now break the agreement with Greenland on the preparation of charts. Although one may have sympathy for the political objective, it is not acceptable to place Danish domestic policy on account of agreements reached between the parts of the Kingdom Community. This is not a sudden external situation, but a deliberate political decision. The breach of the agreement could easily have been avoided when the resignations were received, for example. by postponing the relocation of precisely the posts until the charts were drawn up and the agreement with Greenland was fulfilled. Therefore, the government must take responsibility and find a solution so that the agreement with Greenland on the chart survey can be respected. The Greenlandic justice system also needs to be overhauled, from the working conditions of the municipal officers and police, to the case bumps of the courts, to the terms of service of the sentenced persons. I would like to sign for the solutions to acute problems we have had with the previous government, such as the reopening of the temporary institution in Kangerlussuaq, as well as an extra effort to remove some of the cases. But that is not enough. The lack of political will to give priority to the Greenlandic justice system puts parts of the legal certainty of the citizens of the Kingdom less favourable than that of others. We can do better than that. If Denmark wants to act as an Arctic great power, it requires that it has a good relationship with the Arctic part of the Commonwealth as a starting point. This requires a special effort to promote the responsibilities that are 100 per cent. Danish responsibility. This should be taken into account by the government in the preparatory work of the Finance Law which runs over the summer holidays. In addition, the Arctic Strategy of the Kingdom Community must and must be carried out in close cooperation with the Greenlandic authorities if the ambition to become an Arctic great power is to be taken seriously. In Greenland we have our own elected parliament and our own elected government. The Danish Government should involve our elected representatives much more closely in dynamic cooperation on the policies pursued in the Arctic and foreign policy in general. It is significant for the current way of conducting foreign policy that the Taksøe report did not involve the Greenlandic government or authorities in the preparation of the report until the report was ready, despite the fact that it is Greenland that forms the Arctic part of the Kingdom Community. The next step should therefore be to refine the foreign policy strategy in cooperation with Greenland and the Faroe Islands, so that it can become the whole strategy of the Kingdom community and not just Denmark's strategy. We are part of the world for good and evil, and neither large nor small countries can themselves. The national community is today the third largest NATO country in terms of area. If Greenland one day leaves the Kingdom, Greenland will still be the third largest NATO country in terms of area. Denmark as an Arctic great power is still a dream, and it will therefore require a serious and active commitment on the part of Denmark if we are to become an Arctic great power together in the Kingdom Community. The Kingdom is so much more than just Danishism. These are different stories, different languages, different values and different approaches. This must be our strength and starting point, for then the Kingdom will be something we share, even after Greenland becomes an independent nation. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160601204844	Thank you. Today, exactly one year ago, I agreed to run for the parliamentary elections and can now look back on my first season at Castle. If anyone is in doubt, I have tried to make it understandable to the Danish Parliament that we have a strong independence movement in the Faroe Islands and a expressed desire to cement our right to self-determination. This requires some rethinking about the relationship between Denmark and the Faroe Islands, and I will come back to that. As a Republican, on the other hand, I have largely refrained from interfering in purely Danish matters, because I believe basically that the Danes should make decisions for Denmark and that the Faroe Islands should make decisions for the Faroe Islands. It is important to create an understanding of what is moving in the Faroe Islands, but it is also necessary to see what the situation really is in 2016 and from there to look forward, like both 5, 10 and 20 years • and beyond. There should be good conditions for an up-to-date and constructive dialogue between the countries, because in both countries we had new fresh governments within a few months of late summer. In the Faroe Islands we formed a progressive government that wants to take even greater responsibility for the development of Faroese society, and the Danish government is led by a Prime Minister who has more contact with the Faroe Islands than any Prime Minister before him. But expectations have not been met. Instead of taking a constructive approach to the agenda of the new Faroese Home Rule Government, it seems that Denmark's government would rather lean on the political line of the opposition and the sister party of the Samband Party, which was laid down in 1906. The party represents a constitutional conservatism that could not even be surpassed by the Northern Ireland Unionist Pastor Ian Paisley. This has so far resulted in the Prime Minister and his government sticking to a concrete legal interpretation of the constitution and the unity state when we talk about the broad lines of Faroese constitution and foreign policy manoeuvrability. But on the other hand, the symbolist drum of the so-called Commonwealth is hard hit when it comes to the purchase of 534 Faroese flags, which are to wander from public Danish flagpoles when we have a party and Ólavsøka • which is not our national day, but a national party. During the parliamentary year, we have repeatedly discussed the national government's objective of presenting a constitution, Stjórnarskipan, to the Faroe Islands for a referendum in 2017. Each time we get the same announcement from the Prime Minister's Office, namely that it must be kept within the framework of the constitution and the Kingdom, and that if you want to resign from the kingdom, you can do so. The latter, of course, is meant as a hidden threat to the Faroe Islands if you do not stay within the framework of the Danish Government. I think I speak for most people when I say that the desire for a Faroese constitution is about the cohesion of Faroese society and about Faroese self-determination. We have taken on a great responsibility for our people and their well-being. It is about legislation, it is about administration, it is about economics, it is about Faroese language, it is about Faroese culture, and it is about Faroese independence. It must be properly grounded in some fundamental values and agreements that unite our people and create a framework that we will all appreciate and safeguard. I am convinced that a large majority of the Faroese people are prepared to vote in favour of a Faroese constitution and the Faroese self-determination. On the other hand, I think it is an untimely interference when we repeatedly suspect the motives behind the desire for a Faroese constitution and demand a distinction between a constitution within and outside the framework of the Kingdom Community. I really do not think that the government should wrap up the Faroese will in a pre-defined legal form, which is cast by the Ministry of Justice and the Prime Minister's Office in Denmark. It must be up to the Faroese people to decide for themselves. Denmark has a long tradition of stretching the interpretation of what can be contained within the constitution, so where there is a will, there is also a way. Home government and self-government systems in particular are good examples of this, and I have taken note of two fresh examples, one of which is the law to stop the so-called hate preachers, and the government says that it will challenge the limits of the constitution. And the second is the matter of citizen involvement, where a change in the word right to the word possibility creates the necessary constitutional space. Remember, the Constitution is not about secession, but about strengthening the foundations of the Faroese nation. If the Faroese people decide at a later stage for secession, they will decide whether it will require a different constitution from the one they now want to implement. A few days ago I visited an exhibition at the National Museum of Ireland in Dublin. It bore the name "Proclaiming a Republic": The 1916 Rising, and it was about the Easter uprising in Dublin 100 years ago, which became so important for the formation of the Irish Republic. This was not the first time the Irish rebelled against the British oppressors. The uprising developed into a week-long tragedy in which 500 people perished, most of them civilians, and the seven leaders of the self-appointed Provisional Irish Government were all executed. Thus, the English defeated the rebels with a cash effort and held unbridledly in power. But their steep attitude led to the rebels winning the Irish hearts and, on that basis, six years later they could form the first government in the Irish Free State, which was later proclaimed as a republic. Now I do not expect the situation with the referendum in the Faroe Islands to develop as in Dublin 100 years ago or as in Klaksvík 50 years ago when a warship of policemen was sent to the Faroe Islands to defeat rebellious salmonids. It's all about a constitution, not a secession. But the government's conservative line when it comes to the unity state and the framework of the constitution is not constructive and belongs to the past. It must not and will not prevent us from formulating and deciding a Faroese constitution at our own request. Compared to the government's announcements, however, the debate in the Danish Parliament on the Danish Empire was much more open, nuanced and committed. Most rapporteurs opened a constructive and up-to-date debate on the relationship between Denmark and the Faroe Islands, which was intended not only to ensure the existence of the Danish Kingdom, but also to identify the positions, interests and visions of the countries for the future, and which basis can be created in order to develop a new model of cooperation, a version 2 or 3, if you like. For example, historian Uffe Østergaard has. pointed out that a new federal model of cooperation is needed to replace the 1948 home governance model. By the way, it's retired. And the politicologist Ulrik Pram Gad actually follows up with his thesis that while EU countries are striving for an ongoing adaptation through an ambition to develop an ever-closer relationship, the constant aspirations of the Faroe Islands and Greenland for greater autonomy require a ever-losser model for cooperation with Denmark. And precisely this quest for greater autonomy is the motive behind the often successful development in the Faroe Islands. I will not tire you much more on this wonderful summer day, but let me just mention on the positive list that I recognise the efforts to identify border barriers between countries. Unfortunately, the list is long and growing, and it often hits people quite randomly and unintentionally. A proposal for the reduction of border barriers is that the government helps the Faroe Islands to enter into the Nordic agreements independently, because it is in fact the case that other Nordic citizens, through these agreements in certain contexts, have better protected rights in Denmark than forefathers have, and vice versa. Another suggestion could be that both Danish and Faroese legislators made greater efforts to ensure that countering unintended consequences were dealt with already during the examination of legislative proposals. Experience shows that Denmark's continued integration with the EU has resulted in a process in which the Faroe Islands and Denmark drift farther and farther apart, whether intended or not. As you know, it is difficult to serve two masters at the same time, and it can sometimes be quite complicated to find around in the so-called Kingdom community. One example is that Danes with Danish citizenship who move to the Faroe Islands do not have the right to vote in the European Parliament because the Faroe Islands have chosen to be outside the EU. However, Danes with Danish citizenship who move to a third country that is also outside the EU retain their right to vote in the European Parliament. This shows a specific democratic problem for Danes, and it has arisen from the fact that the relationship between Denmark and the Faroe Islands is not so simple that we can just sit back and enjoy the prospect of a harmonious unitary state, because it does not exist. It could perhaps be solved by a dual citizenship with a Faroese and a Danish passport. At least we had clear lines. But the answer to that is enough that it does not allow the Constitution.

It's nothing new. They also disagreed with these officials when they said that the Faroe Islands could not have their own legislative power or their own tax system or take over the underground because it was a breach of the constitution. It has been shown time and time again that the legal notes of the officials are not stronger than the political will of the people, and fortunately for that. The interesting thing is that this is not just a conflict between the political system in the Faroe Islands and the experts of the Ministry of Justice. It is also a conflict between these experts and all other experts. In fact, there is not one independent public or State lawyer who has dealt with this, who agrees with the people of the Ministry of Justice that the Kingdom Community is fully within the framework of the Basic Law and not one. And that may give rise to a dull suspicion that the notes written are not legal but political, that is to say, that it is policy camouflaged as law. And that's not smart politics. The essence of what you want to do on the first side of the Faroe Islands' constitution is, of course, just the basic principle of democracy, that it is the people of the Faroe Islands who are the Faroe Islands' supreme authority. And it simply cannot be illegal, and it cannot be illegal under a law that certainly applies in the Faroe Islands, but in reality has not been adopted by the Faroese people if we are to stick to the very, very formalistic reading of things. If the position that the officials of the Ministry of Justice have the exclusive right to interpret the Constitution had been in force for the past 60 years, we would not have had a kingdom community today. Fortunately, however, the Kingdom has developed steadily and calmly in good understanding towards a kind of federation, and that is the understanding that we must safeguard in time. This is a positive starting point: increased employment, falling unemployment, a baby boom on the way. And that in itself is good. Interestingly, it is also possible to read that researchers explain the prospect of this baby boom that it is not least due to economic optimism. That's amazing. The sun shines, and the prospective parents are optimistic and give birth to children. They do not do so as never before, but there is the prospect that for the first time in a number of years we will have a birth rate of over 60 000. It is a good circle: falling unemployment, increasing employment, more Danish children. It's a positive start. Denmark is not in crisis, but it does not shake the fact that we who are here now have a responsibility to look forward. Because it should be so that when there is a prime minister here in 34 years' time and I can guarantee that it will not be me and look back, just as I now looked back briefly to the time when Prime Minister Anker Jørgensen left the government offices, one must be able to tell about the same leap in prosperity and welfare development. We have a responsibility, not to give the easy answers, but to provide the real answers needed to ensure that the growth that is underway can be increased, that the room for manoeuvre that has shrunk due to the fall of oil prices, the pressure for refugees and migration, is increased again so that we can develop our welfare societies. Those of us who are here today have a responsibility to ensure that in the future we have the right skills in our labour market, so that our companies, whether private or public, are not hindered by the absence of the right, well-trained workforce. We who are here today have a responsibility to design Denmark in such a way that it is attractive to invest in Denmark, create companies, watch them grow and flourish and not just sell them from when they are mature to it. That is all we have to do in the autumn. That is why I am looking forward to a little in the autumn, but first of all to finishing this collection, getting a high school agreement, a draft law agreement, an agreement on fighter planes, municipal economics agreements and getting the PSP addressed. With that, I would just like to end where I started by saying thank you for a long and a good and a peaceable debate in a good tone.

V 81 by Kristian Thulesen Dahl (DF), Jakob Ellemann-Jensen (V), Simon Emil Ammitzbøll (LA) and Søren Pape Poulsen (KF), and a vote can be taken now. We shall conclude the vote. In favour of voting 59 (DF, V, LA and KF), against 56 (S, EL, ALT, RV and SF), abstentions 0. Proposal for adoption No V 81 adopted. This brings us to the motion for a resolution. V 80 by Mette Frederiksen (S), Pelle Dragsted (EL), Rasmus Nordqvist (ALT), Morten Østergaard (RV) and Pia Olsen Dyhr (SF) lapsed. That concludes the question.
